1.4.5 Evacuation Considerations for Residential (Care Facility) Buildings,
(Purpose Group 2(a)(ii))
1.4.5.1 General
In a residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)), occupants may be totally 
dependent on other people for evacuation. Normal "self-help" evacuation procedures are 
therefore inappropriate, and consideration must be given to designing escape routes to 
facilitate the planned mode of evacuation.
The following provisions allow progressive horizontal escape to be made into adjoining 
compartments in a residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)).
The object is to provide a place of relative safety within a short distance, from which further 
evacuation can be achieved, but under less pressure of time.
Provisions relating to sprinklers in shopping centres are set out in Section 8.
1.4.5.2 Compartmentation
A residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)) should be divided into at least 
two compartments in such a way as to permit horizontal evacuation of each compartment.
An exception to this may be made in storeys which consist exclusively of ancillary 
accommodation (e.g.: kitchen, storage rooms, staff areas, laundry).
1.4.5.3 Designing for Progressive Horizontal Evacuation
In designing a storey which is divided into compartments for progressive horizontal 
evacuation, the following conditions should be observed:
(a) 
Adjoining compartments into which horizontal evacuation may take place should 
each have a floor area sufficient to accommodate, not only their own occupants, but 
also the occupants from the adjoining compartment. This should be calculated on the 
basis of the design occupancy of the compartments.
(b) 
Each compartment should have at least one other escape route, independent of the 
route into the adjoining compartment (see Diagram 11). This other route may be by 
way of a third compartment, provided that compartment contains a storey exit which 
is not by way of another compartment, and which is independent from the exits from 
the other compartments.
Where the above conditions have been met, for the purpose of travel distance (see Para 
1.4.2.2), a door in a compartment wall may be regarded as being equivalent to a storey 
exit.
In an unsprinklered building (see Section 8), the maximum number of bed spaces in a 
compartment should not exceed 10 bed spaces.
46

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.4.5.4 Escape route widths in Residential (Care Facility) Buildings (Purpose Group
2(a)(ii))
In residential (care facility) buildings (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)) an escape route should 
generally be not less than 1.15 m in width and where appropriate, should be suitably 
designed to allow the movement of beds along the escape route.
The width of an exit from any room should not be less than 0.9 m.
The width of a final exit should be not less than 1.15 m.
1.4.5.5 Door-Closing Devices
Fire doors are required to have self-closing devices (see Appendix B). Where self-closing 
doors could present an obstacle to the residents of the building, then the following 
hardware in accordance with I.S. EN 1155 should be provided, either:
(a) 
bedrooms – free-swing door closers; or
(b) 
circulation spaces – hold-open devices.
1.4.5.6 Bedrooms
A wall between a bedroom and any room that is not a bedroom, should be fire resisting in 
accordance with Appendix A, Table 31. This provision applies irrespective of the 
installation of a sprinkler system.
Walls between bedrooms should be fire resisting, in accordance with Appendix A, Table 
31. An exception to this requirement is where the building is provided with a sprinkler 
system in accordance with Section 8. In such a building, a wall between a bedroom and 
another bedroom, need not be fire resisting, but should conform to one of the following:
(a) 
Extend to the underside of the fire-resisting floor or roof above; or
(b) 
Extend to a non-fire-resisting ceiling, with cavity barriers on the line of bedroom walls 
in the void (a), (see Subsection 3.6); or
(c) 
Extend to a fire-resisting ceiling, which extends throughout the compartment (see 
Diagram 42(b)).
A bedroom ensuite may be taken to form part of the bedroom.
47

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.4.6 External Escape Routes
1.4.6.1 External Escape Stairway
If more than one escape route is required from a storey, or part of a building, one of those 
routes may be by way of an external escape stairway, provided that:
(a) 
The stairway does not serve a storey more than 11 m above ground level, and
(b) 
In the specific case of:
(i) 
an assembly and recreation building, the route is not intended for use by 
members of the public; or
(ii) 
a residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)), the route serves 
only office or residential staff accommodation.
External escape stairways should comply with the requirements set out in Para 1.5.9.
1.4.6.2 Escape Over Flat Roofs
If more than one escape route is required from a storey or part of a building, one of those 
routes may be by way of a flat roof, provided that the following provisions are met:
(a) 
The route should not serve a residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 
2(a)(ii)), or a part of a building intended for use by members of the public; and
(b) 
The roof should be part of the same building from which escape is being made; and
(c) 
The route across the roof should lead to a storey exit; and
(d) 
The part of the roof forming the escape route and its supporting structure, together 
with any opening within 3 m of the escape route, should have a fire resistance as set 
out in Appendix A, Table 31; and
(e) 
The route should be clearly defined.
Guidance with respect to the provision of guarding to prevent falls is set out in Technical 
Guidance Document K (Stairways, Ladders, Ramps and Guards).
1.4.6.3 Escape Over a Podium
The provisions of Para 1.4.6.2 also apply to an escape route over a podium which gives 
direct access to a place of safety and where the podium is constructed of materials having 
a reaction to fire classification achieving Class A2 – s3, d2, and having a fire resistance as 
set out in Appendix A, Table 31.
There may be additional fire resistance requirements if this is also the main fire service 
access to a firefighting shaft (see Section 5).
49

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.4.7 Audience and Spectator Facilities
There are particular problems that arise when fixed seating limits people’s ability to 
escape. Any such arrangements made for a closely seated audience (or closely seated 
spectators, including in bleachers) should meet the provisions of the following paragraphs.
For design with respect to handrails and guarding, see Technical Guidance Document K 
(Stairways, Ladders, Ramps and Guards). For design with respect to access and use of 
audience and spectator facilities, see Technical Guidance Document M (Access and Use).
1.4.7.1 Tiered Seating
The number of seats in a row should be in accordance with Table 6.
Seatway widths should be not less than 0.3 m and should be constant throughout the 
length of the row.
Where seats tip up automatically, (see Diagram 12) the seatway width should be 
measured between the back of one seat unit and the maximum projection of the seat unit 
behind when the seat is in the up position.
The slope of a tier of seating should not exceed 35° above the horizontal.
The number of steppings in a tier uninterrupted by cross-gangways should not exceed 40 
if the rake exceeds 25°.
1.4.7.2 Gangways
1.4.7.2.1 Widths of Gangways
Gangways (see Diagram 12) should:
(a) 
be not less than 0.9 m wide where less than 50 persons are expected to use them;
(b) 
be not less than 1.1 m wide where more than 50 persons are expected to use them;
(c) 
not have obstructions which reduce the clear width of the gangway, (handrails which 
do not intrude more than 100 mm into the gangway may be ignored); and
(d) 
maintain a uniform width throughout the length of the gangway. Where the escape 
flow is in one direction only (i.e. access to any alternative means of escape is along 
the rows), the gangway may widen towards the storey exit.
1.4.7.2.2 Design of Gangways
Intersections of transverse and radial gangways in auditoria with tiered seating should 
form a “T” junction.
Connections between transverse and radial gangways should be offset to ensure a 
smooth flow to the exits.
50

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
In stepped tiers, the height of each step in a gangway should be not less than 100 mm and 
should not exceed 190 mm. Where there are two or more rises to each row of seats, each 
step should be of equal height.
1.4.7.2.3 Access to Storey Exits
Storey exits should:
(a) 
be approached from the side by transverse gangways when provided within the 
body of a seating layout; and
(b) 
have a landing which is at least the width of the exit and at least 1.1 m deep 
immediately in front of the exit doors, when approached from a stepped gangway.

(1) Min 0.9m for < 50 persons, Min 1.1m for > 50 persons

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.4.8 Premises Provided with a Stage
Stage areas present particular problems in that they present additional fire risk due to the 
presence of combustible materials. These provisions apply where dedicated stage areas 
with ancillary rooms (scene docks, dressing rooms, etc.) are designed.
These provisions do not apply to stage areas with a low fire load e.g., temporary stages, 
small community / village halls, school halls, lecture halls or similar.
For fire resistance requirements of areas ancillary to the stage, see Para 3.4.4.7.
For sprinkler systems in stage areas, see Section 8.
1.4.8.1 Stage Areas
1.4.8.1.1 Means of Escape from Stage Areas
Escape routes from the stage and the stage basement should comply with Para’s 1.4.1 to 
1.4.4, and travel distances should comply with those listed in Table 3 for “other areas”.
Protected lobbies should be provided between:
(a) 
a stage and the dressing room corridor(s); or
(b) 
a stage and a final exit to the open air; or
(c) 
a stage and the auditorium, when a pass door is provided in a proscenium wall; or
(d) 
a stage basement and the orchestra pit.
1.4.8.1.2 Separation of Stage Areas
A proscenium wall, where provided, should be constructed of materials having a reaction-
to-fire classification achieving Class A2-s3, d2, when classified in accordance with I.S. EN 
13501-1 and having a standard of fire resistance equivalent to that required for the 
elements of construction of the building, but no less than 60 minutes (see Appendix A, 
Table 31). The wall should be carried up from the lowest level of the stage basement to 
the under-side of the roof.
53

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
Where a safety curtain is provided, it should either:
(a) 
conform to the following:
(i) 
be made of material with a reaction-to-fire classification achieving Class A2-
s3, d2, when classified in accordance with I.S. EN 13501-1; and
(ii) 
be capable of withstanding a pressure differential of 0.5 kPa over its entire 
surface; and
(iii) be so fitted that when fully lowered it inhibits the penetration of smoke around
the perimeter of the opening from the stage; or
(b) 
be a smoke curtain tested in accordance with IS EN 12101-1, with a tested 
performance of no less than 60 minutes.
1.4.8.1.3 Grids and Galleries
Any grid and galleries, including lighting galleries and perches, should be of materials 
having a reaction-to-fire classification achieving Class A1.
The working fly gallery(s) and the grid should each be provided with an escape route 
independent of the stage by way of:
(a) 
a storey exit to an external route; or
(b) 
a doorway to another part of the building leading to a storey exit.
1.4.8.2 
Ventilation of Stage Areas
Open stages should be ventilated in accordance with Para 6.9.1.
Separated stages should be ventilated in accordance with Para 6.9.2.
1.4.8.3 
Dressing Rooms
Escape from dressing rooms should be in accordance with the recommendations of 
Section 1. Travel distances should not exceed those given in Table 3.
An alternative means of escape, which is independent of the stage should be provided.
For the provision of fire resisting construction, see Para 3.4.4.7(d).
1.4.8.4 
Scene Docks
Any opening between a scene dock and the stage should be protected by a fire door. 
Scene docks should be ventilated in accordance with Section 6, Para 6.9.1.
For the provision of fire resisting construction, see Para 3.4.4.7(d).
54

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.4.9 Student Accommodation Unit (Purpose Group 2 (b))
A student accommodation building (see Diagram 13), provided with a single escape 
stairway should be acceptable where:
(a) 
it is designed only for the purpose of accommodating students. 
(b) 
Each individual student accommodation unit should be separated from any other part 
of the building by compartment walls and floors, in accordance with Para 3.4.4.4.
(c) 
All habitable rooms should be accessed directly from a protected entrance hallway, 
which delivers to a common protected lobby/corridor.
(d) 
The maximum travel distance in the protected entrance hallway is 9 m, but this may 
be increased to 15 m, where a sprinkler system is provided within the unit.
(e) 
The protected entrance hallway should have the appropriate fire resistance as   
specified in Appendix A, Table 31 and Table 32.
(f) 
All doors forming part of the fire-resisting enclosure of the protected entrance hallway 
should be fire doors fitted with self-closers (a fire door to a hot press does not need a 
self-closer), and have the appropriate fire resistance specified in Appendix B.
(g) 
The walls forming the protected entrance hallway (excluding the compartment wall 
separating the unit from any other part of the building) should:
(i) 
be carried to the underside of the structural floor, or roof above, and be 
appropriately fire stopped (see Diagram 13(b)); or
(ii) 
be carried to a ceiling which is not fire resisting, with cavity barriers placed in 
any void, along the lines of the protected entrance hallway, to limit the spread of 
smoke (see Diagram 13(c)); or
(iii) be carried to a fire-resisting ceiling, which extends throughout the
compartment(see Diagram 13(d)).
(h) 
Any penetrations of the protected entrance hallway should be fire-stopped in 
accordance with Subsection 3.7.
(i) 
The communal area within the student accommodation unit, which may consist of a 
kitchen, dining and sitting area, should be enclosed fire-resisting construction (having 
a fire resistance as set out in Appendix A, Table 31), and should be accessed from 
the dead end of the protected entrance hallway within the unit. No other main cooking 
appliance should be provided within the unit.
(j) 
Escape within the common parts of the building should conform to the relevant 
provisions under Paras 1.6.6, and 1.6.7, including the provisions of Para 1.6.7.1 for 
buildings with a topmost floor level of more than 30 m.
Where the above provisions are not met, the student accommodation should be designed 
as a Hall of Residence meeting the relevant provisions of Subsection 1.4 and 1.5 for 
horizontal means of escape, including the provision of no less than 2 escape stairways 
where any storey has a height greater than 11 m.
For sprinkler systems in student accommodation units, see Section 8.

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5 Design for Vertical Escape
1.5.1 Introduction
An important aspect of means of escape in multi-storey buildings is the availability of a 
sufficient number of adequately sized and protected escape stairways.
This section deals with these matters, including measures necessary to protect escape 
stairways, for all types of building other than hospitals (see Para 1.2.1).
Generally, all upper floors in multi-storey buildings are required to have at least two 
escape stairways. There are circumstances where this principle may be varied, as set out 
under Para’s 1.5.2 and 1.5.3.
1.5.2 Number of Escape Stairways
The number of escape stairways needed in a building (or part of a building) will be determined 
by:
(a) 
where a single stairway is acceptable by virtue of the height or use of the building 
(see Para 1.5.3);
(b) 
where the provisions in respect of a small premises (see Subsection 1.8) applies;
(c) 
the constraints imposed in Subsection 1.4 on the design of horizontal escape routes;
(d) 
whether independent stairways are required in mixed occupancy buildings (see 
Para 1.5.2.2).
In all circumstances, stairs should be adequately sized to facilitate the evacuation of the 
occupants of the building (see Para 1.5.4 and Para 1.5.5).
1.5.2.1 Large Buildings
In large buildings, provisions for access for the fire service may apply. Where firefighting 
shafts are provided (see Para 5.5.5), the stairway within the shaft may be used as a 
means of escape. The number of stairs required in such a building may be affected by the 
requirements of Section 5.
1.5.2.2 Buildings Containing Different Purpose Groups
In buildings containing different purpose groups, escape routes are not required to be 
independent except in the case of assembly and recreational (Purpose Group 5(a) and 
5(b)), or residential (Purpose Group 1(c), Purpose Group 2(a) or Purpose Group 2 (b)) 
uses.
An exception to the requirement for independent escape routes in residential occupancies 
is where it is a building containing flats (Purpose Group 1(c)) and the criteria of Para 
1.5.2.2.1 or Para 1.5.2.2.2 are met.
57

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.2.2.1 Buildings with Not More Than Four Stories
In a building with not more than four storeys above ground or access level, a stairway may 
serve both flats and non-residential (Purpose Groups 3 to 8) uses where:
(a) 
the stairway is separated from each occupancy by protected lobbies (see Para 
1.5.8) at all levels; and
(b) 
an automatic fire detection and alarm system is provided throughout the building 
(see Para 1.9.13).
1.5.2.2.2 Buildings with More Than Four Stories
In a building with more than four storeys above ground or access level, all stairways 
serving the flat or maisonette should not communicate with any other use unless all the 
following are met:
(a) 
there is only one flat or maisonette, which is ancillary to the main use of the 
building; and
(b) 
the stairway is separated from each occupancy by protected lobbies (see Para 1.5.8) 
at all levels; and
(c) 
an alternative escape route is provided from the flat; and
(d) 
an automatic fire detection and alarm system is provided throughout the building 
(see Para 1.9.13).
1.5.3 Single Escape Stairways
The situations where a building (or part of a building) other than a residential (care facility) 
building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)) may be served by a single escape stairway are as 
follows:
(a) 
an assembly and recreation building (Purpose Group 5(a)), or a Day Centre building 
(Purpose Group 5(b)(ii), where there is no storey more than 5 m above ground level; 
or
(b) 
a day centre building (Purpose Group 5(b)(i)), where the premises consists of a 
ground floor storey and one upper storey (first floor level) and the building has been 
designed such that not more than 20 children can be accommodated on the upper 
storey; or
(c) 
In the case of a stairway serving a part of a building containing flats (Purpose Group 
1(c)), which is in accordance with the requirements specified in Subsection 1.6, for 
a single-stairway building; or
(d) 
In the case of all other purpose groups, where there is no storey more than 11 m 
above ground level; or
(e) 
In the case of a basement, the floor of which is not more than 3 m below ground 
level.
In all cases, the single escape route should be in accordance with Para 1.4.2.5.
58

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.4 Width of Escape Stairway
The width of escape stairways should:
(a) 
be not less than the width(s) required for any exit(s) affording access to them; and
(b) 
conform with the minimum widths given in Table 7; and
(c) 
not exceed 1.4 m if serving any storeys more than 30 m above ground level; and
(d) 
not reduce in width at any point on their way to a final exit.
If the width of the stairway is more than 1.8 m, then for reasons of safety in use the 
stairway should have a central handrail. In such a case, the stairway width on each side of 
the handrail needs to be considered separately for the purpose of assessing stairway 
capacity.

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.4.1 Converging Flows at Final Exit
Where a ground-floor storey exit and a stair share a final exit (via a ground-floor lobby), 
then the final exit should be wide enough to evacuate people at a maximum flow rate 
equal to or greater than from the storey exit and stair combined (see Diagram 14).
This can be calculated using the formula: W = ((N/2.5) + (60S))/80
Where
W is the width of final exit in metres
N is the number of people served by ground-floor storey exit
S is the stair width in metres.
If the number of people (N) entering the lobby from the ground storey is more than 60, 
then the distance from the foot of the stair or the storey exit to the final exit (D) should be a 
minimum of 2 m. If that minimum distance cannot be achieved, the width of the final exit 
(W) should be at least the width of the stair plus the width of the storey exit.
Diagram 14 Converging Flows at a Final exit 
Para 1.5.4.1
1.5.4.2 Landings in Residential (Care Facility) Buildings (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii))
In residential (care facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)), any landing associated with a 
stairway forming part of an escape route should be adequate for the purposes of 
evacuation. Any landing associated with such a stairway should have a width not less than 
2.8m and a depth clear of obstructions not less than 1.95 m.
60

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.5 Calculation of Minimum Stairway Width
1.5.5.1 General
Every escape stairway should be wide enough to accommodate the number of persons 
needing to use it in an emergency. This will depend on the number of stairways provided 
and whether the escape strategy is based on the total or phased evacuation of the building 
(or part of the building).
Escape based on total (simultaneous) evacuation should be used for:
(a) 
all stairs serving basements; or
(b) 
all stairs serving buildings with open spatial planning; or
(c) 
all stairs serving residential (care facility) buildings (Purpose Group 2 (a)(ii)) or 
residential (other) buildings (Purpose Group 2 (b)); or
(d) 
all stairs serving assembly and recreation buildings (Purpose Group 5); or
(e) 
all stairs serving shopping centres (Purpose Group 4(b)) (see Subsection 1.7).
Paragraph 1.5.5.3 deals with the concept of total evacuation. Table 8 assumes total 
evacuation of all storeys simultaneously, and may be used for all buildings.
Paragraph 1.5.5.4 deals with the concept of phased evacuation, and sets out the special 
measures that are necessary if a system of phased evacuation is used. Table 9 assumes 
the phased evacuation of not more than two floors at a time, and may be used for any 
buildings except those identified in the (a) to (e) above as needing to be designed on the 
basis of total evacuation.
1.5.5.2 Discounting of Stairways
Whichever method of evacuation (total or phased) is used, where two or more stairways 
are designed as an escape route (whether it is a protected stairs or an open stair – see 
Para 1.4.3.10), excluding buildings containing flats, it should be assumed that one of them 
might not be available due to fire or smoke. It is therefore necessary to discount each 
stairway in turn in order to ensure that the capacity of the remaining stairways is adequate 
for the number of persons needing to escape, unless they comply with Para 1.5.5.2.2.
For buildings listed under Para 1.5.5.2.1, discounting must always be applied.
This discounting requirement applies to a building irrespective of the provision of a 
sprinkler system (see Section 8).
As with the design of horizontal escape routes, where the maximum number of people 
needing to use the escape stairway is not known, the appropriate capacity should be 
calculated on the basis of the occupant capacity. Guidance on calculating occupant 
capacity is set out in Para 1.3.1 and Table 2 of this Technical Guidance Document.
61

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.5.2.1 Buildings Where Stairways Must Be Discounted
Each stairway must be discounted in turn in any of the following cases:
(a) 
assembly and recreation (Purpose Group 5 (a) and (b)) buildings; or
(b) 
any building where, by virtue of the limits on travel distance and/or numbers of 
occupants, only two stairways are required; or
(c) 
any building with a storey more than 20 m above ground level, except a building used 
as an office (Purpose Group 3), or a shop (Purpose Group 4(a)).
1.5.5.2.2 Situations Where Discounting Is Not Required
Excluding buildings listed in Para 1.5.5.2.1 above, stairways are not required to be 
discounted in turn if:
(a) 
every escape stairway is approached on each storey through a protected lobby / 
protected corridor. In such a case the likelihood of a stairway not being available is 
significantly reduced and it is not necessary to discount a stairway. A protected 
lobby need not be provided on the topmost storey; or
(b) 
the stairways are protected by a suitable pressurization system (a design method 
for pressurization of escape routes is set out in I.S. EN 12101-6); or
(c) 
the part of the building that the stairway serves only contains flats (Purpose Group 
1(c)); or
(d) 
the stairway is a single escape stairway which conforms to Para 1.5.3.
Discounting of storey exits still needs to take place, except in the part of a building 
containing flats.
1.5.5.3 
Total / Simultaneous Evacuation
In a building designed for total evacuation, the escape stairways should have the capacity 
to allow all floors to be evacuated simultaneously.
Table 8 gives capacities, based on total evacuation, for stairways of different widths (1m to 
1.8 m) and numbers of storeys served (1 to 10).
Either formula below may be used as an alternative to Table 8 or for more than 10 storeys, 
where the stair width is greater than 1.1 m.
P = 200w + 50(W - 0.3) (N - 1); or
W = (P + 15N – 15) / (150 + 50N).
62

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
Where:
P is the number of people that can be accommodated;
W is the width of the stairway in metres, and
N is the number of storeys served.
Separate calculations should be made for stairs serving basement storeys and stairs 
serving upper storeys.
The population, P, should be divided by the number of available stairs, after discounting.
The formula is useful to determine the width of stairs where people are not distributed 
evenly — either within a storey or between storeys.
The widths of stairways provided should also meet the criteria indicated at Para 1.5.4 and 
take account of the need, where required, to discount any one stairway (see Para 1.5.5.2).

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.5.4 
Phased Evacuation
Phased evacuation may be considered for certain buildings, excluding those outlined in 
Para 1.5.5.1.
The concept of phased evacuation is based on evacuating persons on a sequential basis, 
commencing with those on the storeys most affected by the fire in its initial stages. That is 
the storey on which the fire originates and the one immediately above.
By designing on the basis of phased evacuation, stairway widths less than those needed 
for total evacuation are possible. However, for stairways in such buildings, all of the 
following provisions should be met:
(a) 
The stairways should be approached through a protected lobby or protected corridor 
at each storey except for a top storey consisting exclusively of plant rooms.
(b) 
Every floor should be a compartment floor.
(c) 
If the building has a storey with a floor over 30 m above ground level, the building 
should be protected throughout by an automatic sprinkler system (see Section 8).
(d) 
The building should be fitted with an appropriate fire detection and alarm system to a 
minimum Category L2/L3 standard (see Para 1.9.13).
(e) 
An internal speech communication system should provide communication between a 
control point at fire and rescue service access level and a defined point 
communication point on all storeys.
The minimum width of stairway designed on the basis of phased evacuation is indicated in 
Table 9.
Table 9 Minimum Aggregate Width of Stairways for Phased Evacuation

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.6 Protection of Escape Stairways
1.5.6.1 General
Escape stairways need to have a satisfactory standard of fire protection if they are to fulfil 
their role as areas of relative safety during a fire evacuation. Protection of escape 
stairways is provided by enclosure of the stairway by fire-resisting construction. Certain 
situations (see Para 1.5.8) require the additional protection provided by a protected access 
lobby or corridor to the stairway.
In limited circumstances as set out in this document, such as from a gallery or a raised 
storage area (see Para 1.4.3.10), an open stairway may form part of an escape route. 
Such a stairway is not considered to be a protected stairway, and subject to the provisions 
set out in this document, does not need to be enclosed in fire-resisting construction.
Escape stairways should conform to the provisions in Paras 1.5.6.2 to 1.5.6.8 below.
Requirements for ventilation of stairways are specified under Section 6.
1.5.6.2 Enclosure of Escape Stairways
Every escape stairway, unless it is an external escape stairway (see Para 1.5.9), or unless 
the stairway is permitted to be an open escape stairway, as set out in this document, 
should be situated within a fire-resisting enclosure (i.e. it should be a protected stairway).
There may be additional provisions if the stairway is also a protected shaft (where it 
penetrates one or more compartment floors (see Section 3) or if it is a fire-fighting stairway 
(see Section 5).
The performance requirements for the enclosure to a protected stairway are specified in 
Appendix A (Table 31 and Table 32).
The performance requirements for doors to the enclosure to a protected stairway are 
contained in Appendix B (Table 36).
1.5.6.3 Exits from Protected Stairways
Every protected stairway should discharge:
(a) 
directly to a final exit; or
(b) 
by way of a protected exit passageway to a final exit. The passageway should 
have the same standard of fire resistance, and the same standard of fire protection, 
e.g. provision of lobbies, as the stairway it serves.
1.5.6.4 Separation of Adjoining Stairways
Where two protected stairways (or exit passageways leading to different final exits) are 
adjacent, they should be separated by an imperforate enclosure.
65

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.6.5 Use of Space within Protected Stairways
A protected stairway needs to be relatively free of potential sources of fire. However, the 
following may be included in the area formed by fire-resisting construction enclosing a 
protected stairway:
(a) 
a refuge area which conforms to Para 1.9.14; or
(b) 
sanitary accommodation or washrooms, provided that the accommodation is not 
used as a cloakroom; or
(c) 
a lift that satisfies the provisions for lifts in Para 1.9.9; or
(d) 
cupboards (which incorporate electrical apparatus) that are fire resisting and 
accessed via fire doors, or
(e) 
a reception or enquiry area at ground or access level if:
(i) 
every floor served by such a protected stairway is provided with access to an 
independent means of escape (i.e. an alternative stairway); and
(ii) 
The reception or enquiry area should not be more than 10 m2 in area.
Fixed battery-storage systems should not be installed anywhere within the area enclosed 
by fire-resisting construction forming a protected stairway, or in any room accessed 
directly from a protected stairway.
1.5.6.6 Fire Resistance and Openings in External Walls of Protected Stairways
Where a protected stairway projects beyond, or is recessed from, or is in an internal angle 
of, the adjoining external wall of the building, then the distance between any unprotected 
area in the external enclosures to the building and any unprotected area in the enclosure 
to the stairway should be at least 1.8 m (see Diagram 15).
Further provisions relating to the protection of escape stairways in a residential (care 
facility) building (Purpose Group 2(a)(ii)) are contained in Section 3.
1.5.6.7 Gas or Liquid Fuel Pipes in Protected Stairways
Pipes intended to carry gaseous or liquid fuels, and associated equipment, should not be 
incorporated within a protected stairway.
1.5.6.8 Separation of Places of Special Fire Risk
Escape stairways require a high degree of protection from fire. Rooms or other 
accommodation which have doors opening onto escape stairways can threaten the escape 
routes from a building.
Places of special fire risk (see Subsection 0.10) should therefore be located so that they 
do not communicate directly with the enclosure to a protected stairway.
A lobby should be provided between the place of special fire risk and the stairway, to give 
adequate protection. Such a lobby should be ventilated in accordance with Para 6.2.2.
66

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.7 Basement Stairways
Because of their situation, basement stairways are more likely to be filled with smoke and 
heat than are stairways in ground and upper storeys. Special measures are therefore 
needed in order to prevent a basement fire causing a hazard to upper storeys. These are 
set out in the paragraphs below. For basement stairways in shopping centres, see Para 
1.7.4.
1.5.7.1 Single Stairway
If any upper storey of a building (or part of a building) is served by a single escape 
stairway, then that stairway should not be continued down to serve any basement storey.
Any escape stairway between a basement and the ground storey should be separated, at 
ground level by a protected lobby or protected corridor from the stairway serving the upper 
storeys.
1.5.7.2 More than one Stairway
1.5.7.2.1 Certain Assembly and Recreation buildings
Not more than 50 % of the protected stairways serving the upper storeys or tiers of seating 
in an assembly and recreation building should continue down to basement level. Any 
stairs, which connect with basement levels, should be separated from each basement 
level by a protected lobby. The provisions of this paragraph apply to the following types of 
assembly and recreation buildings:
(a) 
a theatre, public library, hall or other building of public resort used for social or 
recreational purposes; or
(b) 
a place of public worship; or
(c) 
a terminus, station or other facility for air, rail, road or sea travel; or
(d) 
Any other assembly building not listed under Para 1.5.7.2.2 (a) or (b).
1.5.7.2.2 All other uses
If there is more than one escape stairway from an upper storey of a building (or part of a 
building), only one of the stairways serving the upper storeys of the building (or part) need 
be terminated at ground level. Other escape stairways may connect with the basement 
storey(s) if they are separated at each basement level by a protected lobby or protected 
corridor.
This provision also applies to the following types of assembly and recreation buildings:
(a) 
a public house, restaurant or similar premises used for the sale to members of the public 
of food or drink for consumption on the premises.
(b) 
a non-residential school or other educational establishment.
67

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.8 Protected Lobbies and Corridors to Escape Stairways
1.5.8.1 Provision of Protected Lobbies and Corridors
There are situations where an escape stairway requires the added protection of a 
protected lobby or corridor. Except at the top-most level, access to a protected stairway in 
buildings other than a building containing flats (provisions for buildings containing flats are 
contained in Subsection 1.6.) should be by way of protected lobbies or protected corridors 
at all storey levels. This applies in any of the following cases:
(a) 
In a single stairway building of three or more stories (see Appendix C, Diagram 89), 
except in the case in a small guesthouse (see Para 1.5.8.2); or
(b) 
To any stairway where the stairway serves any storey at a height greater than 20 m 
(see Appendix C, Diagram 91); or
(c) 
To any stairway where the building is designed for phased evacuation (see Para 
1.5.5.4); or
(d) 
Between a protected stairway and a place of special fire risk (see Subsection 0.10); 
or
(e) 
In a building where no stairway has been discounted (see Para 1.5.5.2.2 (a)); or
(f) 
Stairways that serve different purpose groups (see Para 1.5.2.2); or
(g) 
To an external stairway, where an external stairs forms part of an escape route, and 
a suitable refuge area is not provided by the external stairs (see Para 1.9.14.1, 
Diagram 41).
1.5.8.2 Small Guesthouses (Purpose Group 2(b)) (Part)
An exception to the requirement for a protected lobby or corridor to the stairway in a 
building served by a single escape stairway may be made in the case of a guest house 
(Purpose Group 2(b)) (part), where all the following conditions are met:
(a) 
The guest house does not contain a basement and there are not more than three 
storeys (see Appendix C - Diagram 89); and
(b) 
There are not more than four bedrooms on any upper storey; and
(c) 
The stairway is a protected stairway (see Para 1.5.6) ; and
(d) 
A fire detection and alarm system is provided in accordance with the requirements of 
Para 1.9.13.3.2; and
(e) 
All habitable rooms are provided with windows which can be used for escape or 
rescue (see Para 1.6.4).
69

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.5.8.3 Requirements for Protected Lobbies and Corridors
Every protected lobby or corridor which is required to be provided should conform to the 
following:
(a) 
Be constructed with walls having fire resistance as required in Section 3 for the 
protecting enclosure of the stairway, as per Appendix A, Table 31, Item 14. Any door 
to the lobby should be a self-closing fire door having a fire resistance not less than 
half    that required for the wall, but not less than 30 minutes.
(b) 
Be constructed so that the clearance between the edges of the doors should be:
(i) 
when in the fully open position is not less than 0.5 m (Diagram 16 “A”); or
(ii) 
when in the closed position is not less than 1.0 m (Diagram 16 “B”);
(iii) or when the distance between any two doors in the closed position is less than
1.0 m, the planes of such doors are at an angle to one another of not less than 
90 degrees (Diagram 16 “C”).
(c) 
Where a lobby leads to a refuge, or a final exit, the minimum distance between doors 
(Diagram 16 “A” or “C”) should be increased to 1.57 m (For further guidance, see 
Technical Guidance Document M (Access and Use)).
(d) 
Be ventilated, if required, in accordance with Para 6.2.2.
Diagram 16 Clearance Between Lobby Doors 
Para 1.5.8.3 (b)(c)
Notes:
(1) For the direction of opening of doors on escape routes, see Para 1.9.3.3

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.5.9 External Escape Stairways
In limited situations (see Para 1.4.6), external stairways are acceptable as forming part of 
an escape route. It is important that the external stairway is sufficiently protected from a 
fire in the building, and in certain circumstances is adequately protected from weather.
An external escape stairway should satisfy the following:
(a) 
The stairway should not serve a storey more than 11m above ground level; and
(b) 
Any part of the external walls identified below should be of fire-resisting construction:
(i) 
Within 1.8 m of, and 9 m vertically below, the flights and landings of a stairway 
leading downwards; or
(ii) 
Within 1.8 m of, and vertically above, the flights and landings of a stairway 
leading upwards; or
(iii) Within 3 m of the escape route from the foot of the stairway at ground level to a
place of safety; and
(c) 
All doors affording access to the stairway should be fire resisting, except that a fire- 
resisting door is not required at the head of any stairway leading downwards and where 
there is only one exit from the building onto the top landing; and
(d) 
Stairways serving floors above 6 m above the external ground level should be protected 
from the effects of snow and ice. The degree of protection required will depend on the 
location of the stairway, proximity of adjacent buildings, and the protection that might be 
afforded to the stairway by the building itself. Such protection may include canopies and / 
or weather screens.
Unprotected openings should not be located in areas identified in (b) (i) – (iii) above, 
excluding a fire-resisting door is not required at the head of any stairway leading 
downwards (see (c) above). Any doors should be fire resisting, and windows should be fire 
resisting and fixed shut.
The construction of external escape stairways should comply with the requirements 
outlined in Para 1.9.4. Diagram 17 shows two examples of unprotected external escape 
stairways.
For requirements of refuges on external stairways, see Para 1.9.14.

an external escape stair.

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.6 Means of Escape from Flats (Purpose Group 1 (c))
1.6.1 Introduction
Guidance on the provision of means of escape (both horizontal and vertical) in buildings 
containing flats (Purpose Group 1(c)), is contained in this section.
There are two distinct components to planning means of escape from buildings containing 
flats:
(a) 
escape within the flat; and
(b) 
escape within the common parts of the building (corridors and stairways) that leads to 
a place of safety.
For sprinkler systems in buildings containing flats, see Section 8.
1.6.1.1 Design Principles
The provisions in this section make the following assumptions:
(a) 
That when a fire occurs within a flat, given the materials and construction used in 
common parts of the building, fire spread beyond the immediate vicinity of outbreak 
should not occur.
(b) 
The design of escape from the flat should not be dependent on external rescue by fire 
services.
(c) 
Simultaneous evacuation of flats in the event of fire is the default position.
(d) 
Occupants of flats are capable of independent self-evacuation, to a refuge or place of 
safety.
1.6.1.2 Application of This Section
Paragraphs 1.6.2 to 1.6.5 deal with the means of escape within each flat. Paragraphs 1.6.6 to 
1.6.7 deal with the means of escape in common areas of the building, and Para 1.6.8 deals 
with common areas in mixed-use buildings.
1.6.2 Internal Planning of Flats
The provisions relating to adequate means of escape within a flat depend on
(a) 
the height of the flat above ground level; and
(b) 
whether the flat is an open-plan flat or a flat with a protected entrance hallway.
1.6.2.1 Flats with an Independent External Entrance at Ground or Access Level
Flats or maisonettes having an independent external entrance at ground or access level 
should be treated in the same way as those having corridor or lobby access.
73

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.6.2.2 Flats with a Protected Entrance Hallway
Flat layouts with a protected entrance hallway can be used in all building types to which this 
section applies (small single-staircase buildings, lobby-, corridor- or open deck approach 
buildings, etc.) irrespective of the storey height of the flat. Normally, habitable rooms should not 
be inner rooms; however where the flat is located close to the ground, or where an alternative 
exit (see Para 1.6.5) is provided, then special rules can apply (see Para 1.6.2.2.2. and Para 
1.6.2.2.3).
1.6.2.2.1 General Provisions
The following provisions apply:
(a) 
The maximum travel distance within the protected entrance hallway should be 9 m. 
This distance should be measured from:
(i) 
the door to the flat, to the entrance of any habitable room, where the flat is on a 
single level (see Diagram 18 (a)); or
(ii) 
the landing of the stairs, where the flat is accessed below the level to the flat (see 
Diagram 18 (b)).
(b) 
The protected entrance hallway should have the appropriate fire resistance as 
specified in Appendix A, Table 31 and Table 32.
(c) 
All doors forming part of the fire-resisting enclosure of the protected entrance hallway 
should be fire doors fitted with self-closers (a fire door to a hot press does not need a 
self-closer), and have the appropriate fire resistance specified in Appendix B.
(d) 
The walls of the protected entrance hallway should:
(i) 
be carried to the underside of the structural floor, or roof above, and be 
appropriately fire-stopped; or
(ii) 
be carried to a ceiling which is not fire resisting, with cavity barriers placed in any 
void, along the lines of the protected entrance hallway, to limit the spread of 
smoke; or
(iii) be carried to a fire-resisting ceiling (see Diagram 9(c), Diagram 42 (b)), which
extends throughout the compartment.
(e) 
All habitable rooms should be accessed directly from the protected entrance hallway, 
unless they conform to the provisions of Para 1.6.2.2.2, or Para 1.6.2.2.3.
(f) 
Any penetrations of the protected entrance hallway should be fire-stopped in 
accordance with Subsection 3.7.
(g) 
If the partitions between a bathroom and any adjacent room has a fire resistance equal 
to that of the protected entrance hallway walls, then the partition between the 
bathroom and the hall need not be fire resisting and the bathroom door need not be a 
fire door.
74

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of Fire
1.6.2.2.2 Flats at Basement Level, or at a Level no More Than 4.5 m above Ground Level
Habitable rooms should not be inner rooms, unless the inner room is provided with a 
window (see Para 1.6.4), or a door (see Para 1.6.5) for escape or rescue purposes.
1.6.2.2.3 Flats Located at a Level More Than 4.5 m above Ground Level
Habitable rooms should not be inner rooms, unless the inner room is provided with a door 
(see Para 1.6.5) for escape purposes.

1.6.2.3 Open-Plan Flats
1.6.2.3.1 Introduction
This section provides means of escape provisions for open-plan flats (see Definitions, 
Subsection 0.10).
The provisions of Para 1.6.2.3 do not apply to small, single-staircase buildings (see Para 
1.6.6.1, Diagram 25 (b)) where a flat is accessed directly from the stairway, which relies 
upon the internal protected entrance hall for lobby protection to the staircase enclosure.
The means of escape requirements in open-plan flats are determined by the maximum 
travel distance within the flat.
Where the travel distance is less than or equal to 9 m, the provisions of Para 1.6.2.3.3  
apply.
Where the maximum travel distance is greater than 9 m, the provisions of Para 1.6.2.3.4 or 
Para 1.6.2.3.5 apply.
75

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
An alternate means of escape from an open-plan flat (where required) can be either an 
escape window direct to the open air, or an alternate exit (door) leading to an exit route 
(corridor and stairs). The appropriate application of these two distinct alternate means of 
escape options depends on the height of the flat above ground level (see Para 1.6.2.3.4, 
Para 1.6.2.3.5, Para 1.6.4 and Para 1.6.5). Escape windows are not appropriate as an 
alternate exit from open plan flats with a floor > 4.5 m above ground level.
1.6.2.3.2 General
Any door to an open-plan flat, from a common area, should have a fire resistance equal to 
the fire resistance of the wall, but not more than 60 minutes, in accordance with Appendix B.

1.6.2.3.3 Open-Plan Flats with a Travel Distance of Less Than 9 m
For open-plan flats of this type (see Diagram 19), the following provisions apply:
(a) 
The flat should not be accessed from the floor above the flat.
(b) 
Any kitchen main cooking appliance should be located remote from the door to the flat, 
and should not prejudice the escape route from the flat.
(c) 
For open-plan flats located at basement level, or not more than 4.5 m above ground 
level, a habitable room should not be an inner room, unless the inner room is provided 
with a window (see Para 1.6.4), or a door (see Para 1.6.5) for escape or rescue 
purposes.
(d) 
For flats located more than 4.5 m above ground level, a habitable room should not be 
an inner room, unless the inner room is provided with a door (see Para 1.6.5) for 
escape purposes.
(e) 
Travel distance within the flat should be measured:
(i) 
from the door to the flat, to the deepest point of any habitable room, or
(ii) 
from the landing of the stairs, where the flat is accessed below the level to the 
flat.
1.6.2.3.4 Open-Plan Flats with a Travel Distance of More Than 9 m Located at 
Basement, Ground or < 4.5 m Above Ground Level
Where open-plan flats of this type are at basement level, or at not more than 4.5 m above 
ground level, any bedroom should be provided with a window (see Para 1.6.4), or a door (see 
Para 1.6.5) for escape or rescue purposes (see Diagram 21). Alternatively, the provisions in 
1.6.2.3.5 may apply.
1.6.2.3.5 Open-Plan Flats with a Travel Distance of More Than 9 m located at any level
Where open-plan flats of this type are located at any level, and the provisions of Para 
1.6.2.3.4 are not followed, the following provisions apply:
(a) 
all habitable rooms should be provided with access to a corridor, which need not be a 
protected corridor. The corridor should provide access to both the main entrance to the 
flat, and an alternative exit from the flat, which should be remote from the main 
entrance to the flat (see Diagram 21 (b)); or
(b) 
the main kitchen / living area of the flat should be separated from the any bedrooms by 
a storey height subdivision which is fire resisting (see Appendix A), and which is 
accessed via a fire door (see Appendix B). Each bedroom should have access to a 
corridor which leads to an alternative exit (see Diagram 21 (c)); or
(c) 
each habitable room, which is an inner room, should be provided with a door that leads 
to an appropriate alternative exit (see Diagram 21 (d)); or
77

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
(d) 
the flat should conform to the following (see Diagram 20):
(i) 
the flat should have a sprinkler system in accordance with Para 8.4.1; and
(ii) 
the flat should be situated on a single storey only; and
(iii) the travel distance from any point in the flat to the final exit from the flat should
not exceed 20m; and
(iv) the kitchen should satisfy one of the following requirements:
I 
It should be enclosed in storey-height construction, which need not be fire 
resisting, or
II 
Alternatively, if the kitchen is not enclosed, the flat layout should be 
designed such that an occupant escaping the flat should not have to 
approach within 1.8 m of the kitchen’s main cooking appliance.

1.6.2.4 
Provisions for Flats with Galleries
Galleries should only be provided in flats with a protected entrance hallway (see Para 
1.6.2.2). A gallery provided with an appropriate alternative means of escape (see Para 
1.6.4 or Para 1.6.5) should be treated as an inner room and conform to (a) of (b) below. A 
gallery with no alternative means of escape should conform to (c) below.
(a) 
For a gallery floor < 4.5 m above ground level, a window (see Para 1.6.4) or door 
(see Para 1.6.5) for escape or rescue should be provided from the gallery level; or
(b) 
For a gallery floor > 4.5 m above ground level, a door (see Para 1.6.5) for escape or 
rescue should be provided from the gallery level; or
(c) 
All of the following provisions (see Diagram 22) should be met:
(i) 
The main entrance and main floor of the flat should be at the same level; and
(ii) 
The gallery is not designed for use as a sleeping area; and
(iii)  The gallery should not project into more than 50% of the area of the room
below; and
(iv) The distance between the foot of the access stair to the gallery and the door
to the room containing the gallery should not exceed 3 m; and
(v) 
The distance from the head of the access stair to any point on the gallery 
should not exceed 7.5 m; and
(vi) Any main cooking appliance within a room containing a gallery should meet one of
the following provisions:
I 
They should be enclosed in storey-height construction, which need not be 
fire resisting; or
II 
the kitchen main cooking appliance should be situated not less than 3 m 
from the stair to the gallery and positioned such that it does not prejudice 
the escape from the gallery.
Diagram 22 Flats with Galleries 
Para 1.6.2.4
80

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.6.3 Maisonettes
A maisonette located at ground level, with accommodation at the ground and the first floor 
only (within the unit) will typically have no floor more than 4.5 m above ground level. 
Therefore, the provisions of Para 1.6.3.1 are appropriate.
A maisonette accessed at any other level (e.g. external stairs, podium, corridor access), or 
with more than three storeys will typically have one or more floors within the unit at more 
than 4.5 m above ground level. Therefore the provisions of Para 1.6.3.2 are appropriate.
1.6.3.1 Maisonettes with no Floors Situated More Than 4.5m above Ground Level
1.6.3.1.1 With an enclosed stair
This type of maisonette should conform to the following:
(a) 
any habitable room which is an inner room should be provided with a window for 
escape or rescue in accordance with Para 1.6.4; and
(b) 
the stairway serving the upper storey should be enclosed with storey-height 
construction, which need not be fire-resisting, and should discharge to the main 
entrance to the maisonette (see Diagram 23(a)).
1.6.3.1.2 With an open stair
An open-plan arrangement, where the stairway rises directly from the ground storey 
accommodation is only acceptable where:
(a) 
all habitable rooms at the upper storey are provided with windows for escape or 
rescue in accordance with Para 1.6.4; and
(b) 
the stairway discharges to within 4.5 m of a door (final exit) at the ground storey 
leading directly to the open air; and
(c) 
the stairway does not discharge into a kitchen (see Diagram 23(b)) and either;
(i) 
any ground storey kitchen, where the main cooking appliance are within 3 m of 
the stairway is enclosed in storey height construction which need not be fire 
resisting (see Diagram 23(c)); or
(ii) 
where the stairway passes within 3 m of the main cooking appliance, the 
stairway should be shielded to the foot of the stairs with ceiling – height 
construction which need not be fire-resisting (see Diagram 23(d)).
1.6.3.2 Maisonettes with a Floor Situated More Than 4.5 m above Ground Level
The maisonette should be designed such that:
(a) any habitable room is not an inner room.
(b) All rooms in the maisonette should be accessed by a protected stairway (see Diagram
24(a)), which should have the appropriate fire resistance as specified in Appendix A, 
Table 31 and Table 32.
81

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
(c) All doors opening into the protected stairway enclosure should be fire doors (see
Appendix B).
(d) Where any floor within the unit is more than 7.5m above the level of the entrance of
the maisonette, a sprinkler system (see Para 8.4.1) should also be provided.
If the partitions between a bathroom and any adjacent room has a fire resistance equal to 
that of the protected stairway walls, then the partition between the bathroom and the hall 
need not be fire resisting and the bathroom door need not be a fire door.
Diagram 23 Maisonettes - No floor more than 4.5 m above ground level 
Para 1.6.3.1
Note:
(1) For provision of fire resisting construction, see Appendix A. 
(2) For provision of compartmentation, see Section 3. 
(3) For provision of fire doors, see Appendix B. 
(4) Open plan arrangements apply only where there is no storey > 4.5m above ground level, and the
main entrance door discharges directly to the open air (see Para 1.6.3.1.2).

(2) For provision of compartmentation, see Section 3 and Appendix A. 
(3) For provision of fire doors, see Appendix B. 
(4) Any doorset forming part of a fire resisting enclosure to an intermediate stairway in a maisonette
should be a fire door.

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
1.6.4 Escape Windows
Windows may provide an alternative means of escape or may be used for rescue 
purposes in flats of limited height.
As an alternative, a door which gives direct access to a private balcony which is suitable 
for rescue by ladder or for escape may be used.
For the design of escape windows, all of the following provisions should be met:
(a) 
The window should have an openable section which provides an unobstructed clear open 
area of at least 0.33 m2. The height should be not less than 450 mm. The width should be 
not less 450 mm. The opening section should be capable of remaining in the position 
which provides this minimum clear open area.
(b) 
The bottom of the window opening should be not more than 1.1 m, and in the case of a 
rooflight not less than 600 mm above the floor, immediately inside or beneath the window 
or rooflight.
(c) 
As an exception to the general guidance in TGD K that guarding be provided for any 
window the cill of which is less than 800 mm in height above floor level (where the external 
ground level is below 1.4 m below floor level), guarding should not be provided to a 
rooflight opening provided in compliance with this paragraph.
(d) 
In the case of a dormer window or rooflight, the distance from the eaves to the bottom of 
the opening section of the rooflight, or, where the window is vertical, the vertical plane of 
the window should not exceed 1.7 m measured along the slope of the roof.
(e) 
The area beneath the window externally should be such as to make escape or rescue 
practicable. For example:
(i) 
where there is a clear drop from a window in an upper storey, the ground beneath 
the window should be suitable for supporting a ladder safely and be accessible for 
rescue; and
(ii) 
where there is a roof, balcony or canopy below a window for escape or rescue, it 
should be structurally adequate, and have a fire resistance, to support and protect 
those using the window.
(f) 
The opening section of the window should be secured by means of fastenings which are 
readily openable from the inside. In certain circumstances, safety restrictors may be fitted 
to such windows (see TGD K Subsection 2.7). Lockable handles or restrictors, which can 
only be released by removable keys or other tools, should not be fitted to window opening 
sections for escape or rescue.
84

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.6.5 Alternative Exits from Flats
Alternative exits from flats, where required for these purposes, should conform to the 
following:
(a) 
Be sited away from the main entrance door to the dwelling such that it can still be 
used as an escape route (see Diagram 21 (b), (c) and (d)).
(b) 
Lead to a final exit or common stair:
(i) 
via a door leading onto an access corridor, common balcony or deck at the 
same or another level; or 
(ii) 
via a protected stairway leading to an access corridor, common balcony or deck 
at another level; or 
(iii) via a door leading onto an external stair or a protected stairway; or 
(iv) via a door leading onto an escape route across a flat roof leading to the head of
a common or external stair.
The internal arrangement of any dwelling having an alternative exit should be such that 
access is possible from all habitable rooms either to the main entrance or to a corridor 
leading to the alternative exit (see Diagram 21 (b) and (c)).
1.6.6 Horizontal Means of Escape in Common Parts of Flats
As seen in Subsection 1.4 and Subsection 1.5, horizontal and vertical escape is 
dependent on the type of building (Purpose Group), on its capacity, on travel distance, and 
on its height. In buildings containing flats, because of the high degree of 
compartmentation, typically, one set of stairs is required. However, the provision of a 
single stairs will limit corridor travel distance, and will require additional provisions, as 
provided for in the following sections.
Means of escape in the common parts of flats can include an open deck approach or a 
common protected corridor/ lobby.
The following sections address means of escape from the entrance doors of a flat to a final 
exit.
The maximum travel distance in a building containing flats will depend on the topmost floor 
height of the building, the provision of a ventilation system and the number of stairs 
provided.
For buildings with a single stairs with a topmost floor level below 11m and no ventilation 
system, the provisions of Para 1.6.6.1 should be met. For all other buildings, the provisions 
of Para 1.6.6.2, or Para 1.6.6.3 should be met. The minimum widths of escape routes 
should conform to Table 5.
85

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire

(6) For stairway ventilation, see Subsection 6.2.
1.6.6.1 Small Single-Stair Buildings
A small single-stair building is a building which does not have a storey at a height greater 
than 11 m, and which has no more than four storeys above ground level. It has a single 
stair and, typically, no smoke control system to the common corridor / lobby. The following 
provisions apply in such a building:
(a) 
The travel distance, measured from the door to the flat to the door to the stairway, 
should be no more than 4.5 m, which may be extended to 7.5 m if a smoke control 
system (see Subsection 6.6) is provided (see Diagram 25(a)); and
(b) 
The stairway should discharge to:
(i) 
the open air at ground or access level, via a final exit; or
(ii) 
a protected corridor that leads directly to a final exit and is provided with the same 
protection from the accommodation as is provided to the stairway; and
(c) 
A stairway may connect to a basement car park, if the access to the car park is 
separated from the stairway enclosure serving the flats, at ground level, by fire 
resisting construction (see Diagram 26) (see Para 6.2.2); and
(d) 
Ancillary accommodation should be separated from the stairway or a single escape 
route by a protected lobby or corridor that is provided with permanent ventilation of 
not less than 0.4 m2 for the control of smoke (see Para 6.2.2) or an equivalent 
mechanical smoke control system.
86

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
Where there are no more than two flats on each floor, the flats may access the stairway 
directly, if the flat is provided with a protected entrance hallway, or a protected lobby, as 
appropriate (see Diagram 25 (b)). Such a protected entrance hallway or protected lobby 
need not be provided if each flat has been provided with an appropriate alternative means 
of escape.

(2) For performance of fire-resisting walls, see Appendix A. 
(3) For performance of fire-resisting doors, see Appendix B.
1.6.6.2 Buildings with a Common Open Deck Approach
An open deck is a common walkway where the area above any required balustrade is 
continuously open to the external air.
The following provisions apply to a building containing flats with an open deck approach 
(see Diagram 27):
(a) 
The floor forming the open deck should achieve 30-minute fire resistance (REI).
(b) 
The open deck should be imperforate. Any service penetration, e.g. drainage pipes 
should be adequately fire-stopped (see Subsection 3.7).
(c) 
The soffit of the open deck should be designed to direct smoke outwards and 
upwards. This may be facilitated by ensuring no down-stand beams are parallel to 
the edge of the deck.
(d) 
The soffit above an open deck having a width of more than 2 m should be provided 
with down-stands placed at 90° to the face of the building on the line of separation 
between individual flats or maisonettes. Down-stands should project 0.3 m to 0.6 m 
below any other beam or down-stand parallel to the face of the building.
87

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
(e) 
Where an open deck provides a single direction of escape (see Diagram 27 (b)), the 
following additional provisions apply:
(i) 
The external wall of the building should achieve at least 30-minute fire 
resistance, up to a height of 1.8 m above the deck level; and
(ii) 
Any window with a cill set more than 1.1 m off the open deck is not required to 
be fire resisting; and
(iii) Doors opening onto the open deck should be E 30 fire doors; and
(iv) The required external balustrade should be imperforate.
There is no limitation on travel distance in open deck approach buildings. However, in 
buildings with a topmost floor level of more than 11 m, provisions relating to maximum 
hose length (see Subsection 5.5.4.1) should be applied.

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
1.6.6.3 Buildings with an Enclosed Corridor / Lobby Approach
1.6.6.3.1 General provisions
A smoke control system (see Subsection 6.6) should be provided to corridor spaces in 
accordance with Diagram 28, Diagram 29, or Diagram 30, as follows:
(a) 
A natural (see Para 6.6.2.1, Para 6.6.2.2) or mechanical (see Para 6.6.2.3) 
ventilation system may be used in buildings with a topmost floor height of less than 
30 m.
(b) 
A natural (see Para 6.6.2.1, Para 6.6.2.2) or mechanical (see Para 6.6.2.3) 
ventilation system may be used in buildings with a topmost floor height of more than 
30 m. The performance of any such system should be proven by fire engineering 
analysis.
1.6.6.3.2 Single direction of travel
In a building or portion of building with a single direction of travel, the following provisions apply:
(a) 
The maximum travel distance, in a single direction (dead end) in a protected corridor 
provided with a smoke control system (see Subsection 6.6), irrespective of the 
topmost floor level, is 7.5 m.
(b) 
Where every flat to a dead-end protected corridor / lobby on the same storey is 
provided with a sprinkler system (see Para 8.4.1), and the same protected corridor(s) 
/ lobby(s) are provided with a smoke control system (see Subsection 6.6), the travel 
distance on that storey can be further extended up to a maximum of 15 m. This 
provision applies to ancillary accommodation on the same storey.
(c) 
Any single direction of travel (dead-end) portion of a common corridor should be 
separated from the rest of the corridor by fire-resisting construction, which includes a 
self-closing fire door (see Appendix B), providing access to the escape stairs (see 
Diagram 29 and Diagram 30).

(b)

entrance door to a flat to the protected stair, or lobby door. Such a lobby should 
provide direct access to the stair and should not provide direct access to a flat or 
ancillary accommodation.
(c) 
Any length of corridor which provides access to two or more storey exits should be 
provided with centrally located fire doors (see Appendix B), where the maximum 
travel distance exceeds 15 m (see Diagram 29, Diagram 30).

1.6.7 Vertical Means of Escape in Common Parts of Flats
An important aspect of means of escape in multi-storey buildings is the availability of a 
sufficient number of adequately sized and protected escape stairways.
1.6.7.1 Number of Escape Stairs
A single stairway is acceptable where the constraints on travel distances (see Para 1.6.6) 
are met.
The provision of additional escape stairs will depend on the constraints on travel distance 
(see Para 1.6.6), and on whether independent stairs are required in mixed-occupancy 
buildings.
For buildings with a topmost floor level > 11 m, additional stairs may be required to satisfy 
the provisions of Section 5 (see Subsection 5.5).
1.6.7.2 Design of Escape Stairways
An escape stairway should conform to Subsection 1.5.
In a building containing flats, the minimum stairway width should be 1.0 m.
In calculating the capacity of stairs, it is not necessary to discount a stair in a multi-stair 
building containing flats, however, the stairway should be appropriately sized for the 
number of individuals expected to use it in an emergency, for a simultaneous evacuation 
building.
A flat should not communicate directly with a stairway enclosure, or a protected exit 
passageway that serves a stair at ground or access level (see Diagram 31). This provision 
does not apply in the case of a small singe stair building (see Para 1.6.6.1, Diagram 25(b)) 
where each flat is provided with a protected entrance hallway, and accesses the stairway 
directly.
In single-stair buildings, an electric meter should not be installed within a common escape 
route unless it is enclosed within a secure cupboard which is separated from the common 
escape route by fire-resisting construction.
Access hatches for services shafts should not be provided within the enclosure to an 
escape stairway, or final exit passageway leading from an escape stairway at ground or 
access level, except where it is a shaft provided for smoke control purposes (see 
Subsection 6.6).
For connections to basements, see Para 1.5.7.
93

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape in Case of Fire
Diagram 31 Final exit from a stairway 
Para 1.6.7.2
Notes:
(1) For compartmentation see Section 3. 
(2) For performance of fire-resisting walls, see Appendix A. 
(3) For performance of fire-resisting doors, see Appendix B.
1.6.8 Ancillary Accommodation
As well as meeting the above provisions (Para 1.6.1 to 1.6.7), ancillary accommodation in 
buildings containing flats (Purpose Group 1(c)) should also meet the provisions as 
specified in this section. Ancillary accommodation should meet the following provisions:
(a) 
For travel distances, number of escape routes, widths of escape routes, etc. for 
ancillary accommodation designed for exclusive use by residents of the flats, the 
relevant provisions of Subsection 1.4 should be applied.
(b) 
In a small single stair building (see Para 1.6.6.1) ancillary accommodation should not 
connect with any part of the only escape route from one or more dwelling(s) on the 
same storey as the ancillary accommodation unless it is separated from the stairway 
by a protected lobby or corridor that is provided with permanent ventilation of not less 
than 0.4 m2 for the control of smoke (see Para 6.2.2) or an equivalent mechanical 
smoke control system.
(c) 
In a building with a single stair and an enclosed corridor / lobby approach (see Para 
1.6.6.3.2), ancillary accommodation should only communicate with a ventilated 
portion of corridor, and should be separated from the common lobby or corridor by 
means of a protected lobby.
(d) 
In multi-stair buildings (see Diagram 32), in any dead end portion of travel, ancillary 
accommodation should be separated from a protected lobby or protected corridor by 
means of a dedicated protected lobby.
(e) 
If the stair serves an enclosed (non-open-sided) car park or a place of special fire 
risk, the lobby or corridor should have not less than 0.4 m2 permanent ventilation or 
be protected from the ingress of smoke by a mechanical smoke control system.
94

Section 1: Means of Warning and Escape In Case of  Fire
(f) 
Communal rooftop areas, designed for the exclusive use of the occupants of the 
building should have travel distances as set out under Section 1.4. The structure 
forming the rooftop should be fire resisting in accordance with Appendix A. A sounder 
linked to the common fire detection and alarm system (see Para 1.9.13) should be 
provided to the rooftop area.
For refuge areas in buildings containing flats, see Para 1.9.14.3.
